          Not done: the same links elsewhere a genre shows as text (the album peek, the album page, the mini player's
          optional genre line) - say if you want them.

  1.29.194 — 2026-10-08 — New: songs are matched to Spotify (and optionally YouTube) while a release is being added. Accept a tracklist and the links are filled in, with scores, for you to review before adding.

          What happens: as soon as songs are in the Add form's tracklist — you accept the Spotify/MusicBrainz
          tracklist banner, the form opens with a tracklist, or (as a last pass) you press Add to crate — the Match
          songs engine runs on them:
            - confident matches (score 85+) are written into the EMPTY Spotify boxes, marked with a green left edge
              and a score badge (hover for the reasons); a likely-but-unsure match gets an amber "?" you can click to
              review; a status line above the tracklist says how many songs were linked and how many are left, with a
              Review... button;
            - Spotify is free so it is automatic; YouTube costs API quota (about 100 of the day's 10,000 units for the
              playlist route) so it runs only if you turn it on;
            - links you typed are never overwritten; a row whose title you changed while it was matching isn't
              filled in; a late answer for a release you've moved on from, or a form you closed, is dropped;
            - the new "Match songs..." button (next to "+ Add track") opens the same review dialog as for saved
              releases, on the unsaved one ("draft" mode): the answers already fetched are reused, and its Save writes
              into the form's boxes — nothing is sent to the backend until you add the release (typing over a matched
              link makes it yours again);
            - Add to crate does one last pass for songs that were typed in or changed and haven't been matched
              (the button reads "Matching songs...", capped at 25 seconds, only when something changed since the last
              run, and any failure just saves what is in the form) and the confirmation says how many were linked:
              "Added to your crate · 8 of 10 songs linked on Spotify".
          Lengths now travel with a tracklist: MusicBrainz's and Spotify's offers include each song's length, kept on
          its row, because length is the strongest sign of the same recording (a song whose length disagrees by a few
          minutes is left for review instead of being filled in).
          Settings, in the ⚙ menu next to the unlinked-song flag: "Match songs when adding a release" (on) and "...also
          on YouTube (uses API quota)" (off), both saved with your other preferences. Without Spotify set up it says
          so and adding is unaffected.

          Also fixed: in the Match songs dialog, previewing a YouTube candidate could be queued behind ANOTHER open
          album's YouTube links (the player builds its queue from whatever tracklist is open unless told otherwise) and
          so play the wrong video; it now passes an empty list, so it plays just that video.
          Cosmetic: the status line no longer inherits the form's all-caps label style.
          Caught by the menu-stacking test: the two new ⚙ menu items made the menu tall enough to run underneath the
          bottom tab bar on a phone (400 of 6,400 sample points covered). The menu is now capped to the room actually
          available (viewport minus the header, and on a phone the bottom bar and player) and scrolls inside, so it
          can't overflow however many settings are added later.

          Verified in real Chromium with Spotify, YouTube and MusicBrainz stubbed: 44 browser checks (pw_add_match.py)
          — accepting a tracklist (lengths kept, five filled, the length-mismatched song left for review, album-first,
          no YouTube requests), the draft review (answers reused, nothing sent, typed-over links), Save (no repeat run;
          every song saved with its link; the confirmation), typed titles matched on Save (button state, held
          requests), the settings, never overwriting, a form opened with a tracklist, three race cases, YouTube on
          (3 requests, 102 units), no Spotify, Spotify erroring, the preview fix, a phone. Removing the
          never-overwrite guard or the moved-on guard each makes tests fail. NOT verified against the real services.
          Not done: the Edit form's tracklist (only Add), saving the lengths with the new release (so a saved
          release's later Match songs has no length clue unless it has been set), and picking the Spotify album for
          the tracklist OFFER with the same scoring (it still takes Spotify's first hit, as before).

  1.29.193 — 2026-10-08 — Genre pages on a phone: each genre row now has an (i) button that opens the genre's page, and a long-press opens the genre menu. Before, there was no way to reach them on a touch screen.

          The problem: the genre page (description, years chart, releases) opened from a RIGHT-CLICK menu. A phone
          has no right-click, and iPhone Safari doesn't turn a long-press into one either; the app's long-press
          helper (attachLongPress, used for album covers) had never been applied to genre rows. So on a phone a
          genre row could only filter the grid.

          Now, on a touch screen (any display with no hover, or one up to 900px wide):
            - every genre row in the Music tab's genre list (open it from the ☰ drawer) has a small round (i) button
              that opens that genre's page directly. It is a proper button with a label ("Open the Progressive Rock
              genre page"), a larger invisible touch target around it, and it doesn't ALSO filter the grid or play
              the genre (a double-tap on it is swallowed). The pinned "All Genres" row has none (it isn't a genre).
              A desktop with a mouse doesn't show it — right-click still does the job there;
            - a half-second HOLD on a genre row opens the same menu right-click does (View genre description, Play
              all N, Shuffle, Edit description). Sliding your finger while holding (scrolling the list) cancels it, a
              quick tap still just filters, and lifting after a hold doesn't also "tap" the row. The genre page's own
              genre tree has the long-press too;
            - opening a genre from the drawer now closes the drawer, so you see the page rather than the list.

          Verified in Chromium at phone size with simulated finger events: 21 checks (pw_genre_phone.py) — the
          button on every genre row and what it does (opens the page, closes the drawer, no filtering, no playing,
          double-tap swallowed), the long-press menu and its actions, a quick tap, a drag, the genre page's tree, and
          that a desktop is unchanged. Removing the long-press makes two checks fail. NOT tested on a real iPhone or
          Android device — only simulated touch events — so the half-second hold is worth trying by hand.

  1.29.192 — 2026-10-08 — Fix: the MusicBrainz histogram couldn't be built because the date search wasn't understood. It now works out which way of writing a date MusicBrainz accepts, and shows exactly what it said if none works.

          What happened: the histogram asks MusicBrainz for release groups by first-release year with
          firstreleasedate:197* (a trailing wildcard). I couldn't test that against the real service, said so in
          the 1.29.190 notes, and the real service evidently didn't answer it usefully, so no dated counts came
          back and the page (correctly) refused to draw a chart from numbers that didn't add up.

          The fix doesn't guess a different syntax; it finds out. Four ways of writing a date are known, in this
          order: a prefix (197*), a range of years ([1970 TO 1979]), a range of full dates ([1970-01-01 TO
          1979-12-31]) and a range of timestamps. On the first Load, each is tested WITHOUT any genre in the query:
          two different years of the 1990s must each come back with at least 3,000 release groups (any single
          year has far more). The first way that does is used for the whole histogram and remembered for 30
          days. A way that is refused (HTTP 400) or accepted but answers 0 or nonsense is skipped; a test costs
          two requests per way (one if it is refused outright), so at most about eight, once.
          If NONE works the panel says so and lists every query it tried with what MusicBrainz answered (the
          status and a snippet of the reply), under "What MusicBrainz answered", with a link to run the tag
          search yourself. That evidence is what's needed to fix it properly. If no request gets any answer at
          all, it's reported as a connection problem, not blamed on the dates. "Try again" and "Refresh" start
          over, including the test. When the way is fine but the genre's own dated counts still contradict its
          total, the page says that instead and shows the numbers it saw.

          Verified (MusicBrainz stubbed to behave each possible way): 38 unit checks of the syntaxes, the query
          builder and the picker (test_mb_hist.js) and 69 browser checks (pw_genre_mb_years.py): the first way
          works (2 test requests); the first is refused and the next found (3); a way that answers 0 is
          rejected (6); only the last works (5); none works (4 requests, the evidence listed, nothing remembered,
          and "Try again" succeeds once it does); the network is down; the way works but the tag's counts don't
          add up; remembered across a reload and re-checked after 30 days; a second genre doesn't repeat the test.
          Mutation-checked: accepting a way that answers 0 makes a test fail.
          STILL NOT VERIFIED against the real MusicBrainz: whether any of the four ways works. If you press
          Load and it still can't build the chart, open "What MusicBrainz answered" and send me what it says.

  1.29.191 — 2026-10-07 — New: Match songs. One dialog finds Spotify and YouTube links for every song of a release at once, scoring each candidate; the automatic Spotify match at play time now picks the right recording instead of Spotify's first result.

          The problem: matching was one song at a time, only inside the Edit dialog's tracklist builder. Spotify
          gave ten unranked results per song with no lengths; YouTube cost 100 of the day's 10,000 quota units
          per song and gave ten unranked results; and playback of a song with no saved link silently played
          whatever Spotify listed first — which can be a live take, a karaoke version or a cover.

          Match songs (a dialog for the whole release). Open it by clicking a red ! (a release's, or a song's),
          from a release's right-click menu ("Match songs to Spotify / YouTube..."), or the "Match songs..." chip on
          the album page. A red ! is now a button (keyboard too).
            - SPOTIFY is matched ALBUM-first. Up to four candidate editions are read in full (the edition you
              linked yourself is always one of them), the one that best explains your tracklist is chosen (a
              standard edition beats a deluxe that contains the same songs), and songs are paired to its tracks by
              title, length and position, one-to-one. Songs it doesn't explain are searched for one by one (free),
              scored the same way. Reading the albums needs the Spotify Client ID/Secret the app already uses.
            - YOUTUBE is matched PLAYLIST-first. The album's playlist (usually the artist's auto-generated "Topic"
              one) is found and read in one go: a playlist search (100 quota units), its items (1) and the videos'
              lengths and whether each can be embedded (1) = about 102 units for the whole album, not 100 per song.
              Searching songs one at a time is offered separately, with the cost stated ("about N x 100 quota
              units; X used today"), and stops cleanly if the quota runs out. A running count of this app's quota
              use is kept per day (YouTube resets at midnight Pacific). A video that can't be embedded is shown as
              weak with the reason (the app plays YouTube in an embedded player).
            - SCORING (0-100) on title (55), artist (15), length (+/-20, the best single clue to the same
              recording), album (10) and track position, minus a penalty for version words the song itself doesn't
              carry (live, remix, acoustic, karaoke, demo, cover, slowed/8D, reaction...). >= 85 confident, >= 65
              likely, else weak; a title that is only similar ('Julia' / 'Julie') can never be confident; the
              same title by a different artist never is either. YouTube's "Artist - Song" titles are matched on the
              song (previously a dash rule deleted the song and kept the artist). Each score's tooltip gives the
              reasons.
            - REVIEW: one row per song with its length, the matched track (artist, album, length, score), a preview
              button (Spotify in the app's player, YouTube in its floating player), and "Others" (alternatives,
              or paste your own link). Confident matches are ticked for you, only for songs with no usable link of
              that kind: a link you already have is never replaced unless you tick a different one. Closing asks only
              if you have changed a selection yourself. ONE Save writes every ticked link of both platforms in a
              single update (the button says "Save 6 Spotify + 5 YouTube links" when both have ticks), and the
              red ! flags clear as links land.

          The automatic match at play time: when a song has no saved link, the Spotify candidates are now RANKED with
          the same scoring (context: the release's album title and the song's length when known) instead of using
          Spotify's order; its order still decides ties. "Try next match" and "Confirm match" use the same order, so
          what you hear is what they refer to. Candidates now carry their length.

          Two older things fixed on the way: the base .arrange-modal rule (max-width 420px) comes later in the
          stylesheet and was beating same-specificity overrides, so the UPDATE LOG dialog added in 1.29.185 has been
          420px wide instead of its intended 760px — both dialogs now chain the class to win (and the update log's
          test asserts its width); and the red ! flags' tests now expect a button.

          Verified: 57 unit checks of the engine (test_song_match.js) and 83 browser checks with Spotify and
          YouTube stubbed (pw_song_match.py): every way in, album-first matching (standard over deluxe, karaoke album
          skipped, no per-song search when the album explains everything), one-update Save, existing links
          untouched, per-song fallback, the YouTube playlist (exactly 3 requests, 102 units), non-embeddable
          video, per-song search with its cost, quota persistence and daily reset, missing credentials, errors,
          a failing save, a phone. Three rules mutation-checked (never replace a link you have; look-alike titles
          never confident; automatic matching back on Spotify's order — which plays the live take and the cover).
          Bugs the tests caught and I fixed before release: the "Artist - Song" shape, a blocked video vanishing
          instead of showing as weak, the Topic-channel bonus never applying to artists starting with "The", and
          the dialog being squeezed to 420px.
          NOT verified: the real Spotify and YouTube services (no network here). The endpoints used are search,
          albums/{id} (Spotify) and search, playlistItems, videos (YouTube). Spotify's developer rules have been
          changing, so if its album endpoint answers differently the dialog says "Spotify answered NNN" with a Retry.
          Not done: matching other sources (SoundCloud), choosing YouTube over Spotify when both exist (playback
          order is unchanged: a Spotify link wins), and a "match every release" bulk mode.

  1.29.190 — 2026-10-07 — Genre pages: the Years active chart can now show MusicBrainz's releases by year as a histogram, on its own or overlaid on your crate (switch: Your crate | Both | MusicBrainz).

          How it works: MusicBrainz has no "releases per year" table, but a search reports its total for any
          query and a query can carry a date, so the histogram is built by asking it. A release GROUP (an
          album/EP/single with its reissues counted once) is counted under the year of its FIRST release, for
          groups tagged with this genre's name: tag:"progressive rock" AND firstreleasedate:1972*. The same
          number as the header's "N on MusicBrainz", broken down by year. It is asked for only when you press
          Load, because it is dozens of requests through the app's one-a-second MusicBrainz queue:
            1. every decade from 1900 to the current one (13 requests);
            2. the years inside each decade that has anything — stopping a decade as soon as its years add up
               to its total, and working out a full decade's last year by subtraction instead of asking.
          That is typically 50-60 requests (about a minute) rather than one per year (77+). The result is
          remembered for 30 days; Refresh reads it again.

          Behaviour: one request at a time, each awaited before the next is queued, so other MusicBrainz work
          (art, adding a release, another genre's count) slots in between instead of waiting behind the whole
          run. A progress panel shows the request count with Cancel; Cancel, closing the genre's tab, or a
          failure stops it, and Resume/Retry carries on from where it stopped — every answer is kept in one
          shared object, so nothing already fetched is asked again. Another genre's tab can be opened while it
          runs, and it keeps going. In "Both" the MusicBrainz bars are teal behind your gold ones; "MusicBrainz"
          alone hides yours. Each series is scaled to its OWN busiest year (shapes, not sizes), tooltips give
          both numbers ("1972 · 2 in your crate · 210 on MusicBrainz"), the axis spans the combined range, and
          the choice is saved with your other preferences. Clicking a year still filters YOUR releases below
          (a year in which you own nothing does nothing). While MusicBrainz is shown, "Compare to a genre" steps
          aside. The summary under the chart: total tagged, how many have a first-release year (and their
          span), how many are undated, before 1900 or not yet released, with a link to run the same search on
          MusicBrainz yourself.

          Sanity check: when a run finishes the numbers are checked against each other. A tag that MusicBrainz
          says has releases but none with a date (or dated counts well above the total) means its date search
          didn't answer the way this expects, and the page says so — with the link — instead of drawing a flat
          chart or calling it done; such a result is never remembered.

          IMPORTANT, NOT VERIFIED: no network here, so the date syntax (a trailing * on the first-release date,
          chosen because it matches however the field is indexed) is untested against the real MusicBrainz.
          Everything else is tested with MusicBrainz stubbed to a known table. If the real service answers
          differently, the page will say so ("didn't return counts that add up") rather than show wrong numbers.
          Not done: a baseline of ALL of MusicBrainz per year (to show a genre's share), counting sub-genres
          (the tag is matched exactly, as for the header figure), releases before 1900.

          Verified in real Chromium: 49 browser checks (pw_genre_mb_years.py) and 22 unit checks of the
          algorithm (test_mb_hist.js). The algorithm test confirms the result is exactly the stubbed table, the
          request count is exactly what the rules imply (57 for the test data) with none repeated, and that
          resume/failure repeat nothing. Two behaviours were mutation-checked (ignoring a closed tab; accepting
          answers that don't add up). The test also found and I fixed a real bug before release: reloading with
          MusicBrainz mode saved and numbers cached crashed the genre page while drawing (the summary read a
          "dated" total that was only set by a fresh load); it is now derived from the stored years.

  1.29.189 — 2026-10-07 — New: a red ! on any release that has songs with no link, with a setting in the ⚙ menu (Checks > "Flag releases with unlinked songs") to turn it on or off.

          What counts: a release whose tracklist you typed by hand is played from the link saved on each
          SONG (Spotify, YouTube or SoundCloud — in the tracklist builder or under "Edit track
          properties..."). The player never guesses for these: a song with no usable link is skipped
          ("Skipping ... no link saved"). So a hand-made release with any such song will play with holes in
          it, and now says so. A link that is there but can't be played (the player's own parsers reject it
          and skip the song just the same) counts as missing. Releases whose tracklist comes from
          iTunes/MusicBrainz are matched to Spotify automatically at play time and have no per-song links to
          be missing, so they are never flagged; nor is a hand-made release with no songs typed in.

          Where it shows: a red circle in the top-right of the cover (Covers, Albums grid and list view), a
          small one before the title in the left-panel album rows (before, so a long title that is cut off
          can't hide it), the release's tiles on the artist and genre pages (and the genre page's list rows),
          and in the quick peek and the album page both beside the release's title AND on the individual
          songs that lack a link, so you can see which to fix. Hover for the wording: "2 of 4 songs have no
          usable link — playing this release skips them. Missing: Gap Two, Gap Four." (or "None of the 3
          songs has a usable link ...", or for one song "...skips it.").

          The setting: the flags are always worked out and the setting only HIDES them (a class on <body>),
          so switching is instant everywhere with nothing redrawn. On by default; saved with the other
          preferences; the menu entry shows a tick when on.

          Kept in step: each flag sits in a slot keyed to its release, and refreshLinkFlagsFor(item) updates
          every slot on screen when a song's links change (updateTrackData — "Edit track properties..." —
          and saveManualTracklist, the quick add/remove-track actions). Adding the missing link clears the
          ! on the card, left row, tiles and album page at once; removing one brings it back. The Edit Album
          save already redraws everything. The quick peek's SONG rows are drawn when it opens and aren't
          updated live (its title flag is).

          It can't break drawing: it runs while every card is drawn, and the app's own link lookup throws on
          an odd tracklist (a null entry from a hand-edited sheet cell, say), so a release that can't be
          judged is simply not flagged — found by the unit test, which first crashed on exactly that.

          Verified in real Chromium: 47 browser checks (pw_link_flag.py) and 24 unit checks
          (test_link_flag.js). The central one: for five releases, the songs the PLAYER ACTUALLY SKIPS when it
          plays them are exactly the songs the flag names. Also: every view above, the left-panel row of a
          long title, the peek and album page per-song flags, the setting on/off/persisted across a reload
          and defaulting on, live refresh both ways, odd data, a phone. Two rules mutation-checked: counting
          a broken link as fine fails a check; skipping the refresh fails four.
          Also fixed in my own genre-page test: its "double-click plays without opening the album page" check
          looked for a #deck element that doesn't exist (the album page is #deckOverlay), so it could never
          fail; it now checks the real thing.
          Not done: flags in the flat Tracks / Album-and-Tracks table views, and flagging releases that
          have no tracklist and no way to play (see the note on what counts above) — say if you want either.

  1.29.188 — 2026-10-07 — Genre pages now show how many releases MusicBrainz has under the genre ("40,474 on MusicBrainz"), linked to its tag page, beside the count of what's in your crate.

          What it asks: MusicBrainz has no "releases per genre" endpoint, but a release-group search by tag
          reports a total however few results are requested, so ONE request with limit=1 answers it:
          release-group?query=tag:"<genre>"&limit=1. A release group is an album/EP/single with its reissues
          counted once — the nearest thing MusicBrainz has to "releases" in your sense. Be aware what the
          number is: release groups TAGGED with that name by MusicBrainz users (so approximate, and a
          tag-and-genre-name match, not a curated total), and it does NOT fold in sub-genres. The link's
          tooltip says so.

          Behaviour:
            - asked once the genre page is drawn, never before; the rest of the page doesn't wait for it. While
              it's out the line says "checking MusicBrainz…";
            - one request per genre, then remembered for 30 days (localStorage, crate_genre_mb_count_v1);
              switching tabs, redrawing the page and reloading ask nothing more; an older answer is asked for again;
            - it goes through mbFetch like every other MusicBrainz call, so it joins the app-wide one-at-a-time
              queue and rate limit; asking again for a genre that's still loading sends nothing extra;
            - an answer for a genre you've since left is kept but never painted over the one you're on;
            - a genre MusicBrainz has no tag for says "not tagged on MusicBrainz" (not a link);
            - a failure (network, 429/503, an answer with no count) says "MusicBrainz count unavailable" with a
              Retry button. It is NEVER remembered and NEVER retried automatically, even when you switch away and
              back, so a flaky connection can't become a request loop;
            - the genre name is lower-cased and its quotes/backslashes escaped inside the search phrase.
          Not done: a "your share" figure (10 of 40,474), counting sub-genres, or using MusicBrainz's own genre
          list (it has no counts).
          Verified in real Chromium, MusicBrainz stubbed (36 checks, pw_genre_mb.py): the figure and link, one
          request, caching and a 30-day expiry across a reload, no repeats on tab switches, zero, loading, the
          slow-answer case (one check fails if the guard is removed — mutation-checked), three kinds of failure
          with Retry, escaping, large numbers, a phone. NOT verified: the real MusicBrainz service (no network
          here), so the exact numbers it gives for your genres are untested.

  1.29.187 — 2026-10-07 — New: genre pages. Right-click a genre in the left panel > "View genre description" opens it as its own tab: a Years active chart, your description (editable, with other genres linked), counts, where it sits in the taxonomy, top artists, and its releases.

          The right-click menu (on a genre row in the Music tab's genre list AND in the genre tab's own tree):
            View genre description (new tab) · Play all N releases · Shuffle · Edit description...
          N counts sub-genres, as the left panel's genre filter does. Shuffle switches the Shuffle setting on.

            - a breadcrumb (Genres > Rock > Progressive Rock; first parent when there are two) and the name,

  1.29.186 — 2026-10-06 — Fix: a red "[UNHANDLED PROMISE REJECTION] Failed to fetch" banner appeared across the top of the page during the Add release search. It was the page's leftover diagnostic overlay reporting a browser extension's copy of a failed request, not a bug in the search.

          What happened (from a screenshot of the banner): while the release search draws its
          results, each row asks the Cover Art Archive for artwork (buildReleaseResultRow ->
          resolveCoverArt -> getCaaJsonUrls). For a release with no artwork that request can
          fail at network level ("Failed to fetch"). The page handles that correctly: its own
          try/catch swallows it, the row keeps a blank thumbnail, and the results loaded fine
          in the screenshot. The banner came from elsewhere. The topmost stack frames were
          chrome-extension://…/libs/requests.js, a browser extension that wraps fetch and
          leaves its OWN copy of the failed request rejecting with no handler. The page can't
          catch that, and the page's TEMPORARY DIAGNOSTIC OVERLAY (a script at the top of
          <head>, added to chase a phone-only load crash and meant to be removed once that
          was fixed) printed it full-width as if it were a crash, with no way to dismiss it.

          Fix: the overlay stays (it is still the only way to see a crash on a phone with no
          devtools) but is now quiet about things that aren't crashes:
            - errors/rejections whose stack is inside a browser extension (chrome-, moz-,
              safari-(web-)extension://);
            - routine network failures ("Failed to fetch", Safari's "Load failed", Firefox's
              "NetworkError when attempting to fetch resource", ...): offline, blocked, or a
              CORS failure from a third-party API;
            - the browser's harmless "ResizeObserver loop" notice.
          Those are console.warn'ed instead ("[CRATE] ignored (...)"), so they are hidden from
          the page but not from devtools. Genuine errors still show. Identical repeats collapse
          into one line with a count, "(×3)". The box now has a ✕ to dismiss it, and a new
          genuine error brings it back. Text is shown as text, never as HTML.
          Also: the two best-effort background fills on a result row (cover art, release
          date) now end in .catch(() => {}). They don't reject themselves, but a throw in
          their follow-up step would have caused the same banner.

          Reproduced before fixing: an extension-style fetch wrapper (a handler-less .then()
          chained onto the real promise) plus failing Cover Art requests gives exactly the
          reported banner. The new test (pw_errbanner.py) fails 10 of its 19 checks on the old
          code and passes all 19 now. NOT verified: the actual extension you have installed,
          or the real Cover Art Archive response (no network here); the reproduction imitates
          what the stack trace shows the extension doing.
          If you'd rather the overlay were removed entirely, or only appeared with a ?debug
          flag, that's a one-line change.

  1.29.185 — 2026-10-06 — New: an Update log. ⚙ > "Update log…" (or click the version at the bottom of that menu) lists every release, newest first, with a one-line summary each and the full notes under "Details".

          Where it comes from: the maintenance log at the top of this file, read back out of
          the page itself — there is no second copy to keep in step, and the release routine
          ("add an entry to the top of the log") is unchanged. The header comment now spells
          out what the log depends on: each entry starts with a line of the exact form
          "  X.Y.Z — YYYY-MM-DD — summary", the FIRST paragraph is the summary the list shows
          (cut at ~260 characters on a word boundary), everything after the first blank line
          becomes "Details", and no line of equals signs inside an entry.

          What's in it:
            - every update, grouped under month headings, newest first. Ordered by VERSION
              rather than file order (the file has 1.29.159 above 1.29.160), compared
              numerically (1.100.0 > 1.10.0 > 1.9.0);
            - "Details" opens the full notes; "Expand all" / "Collapse all" for the lot.
              The details text is built the first time an entry is opened. The file wraps
              its paragraphs at ~80 columns, which reads as ragged half-lines on a phone,
              so the wrapped lines of ordinary paragraphs are re-joined; bullets, indented
              blocks and column-aligned lines are left exactly as written;
            - a search box over versions, dates, summaries AND the full text; when a hit is
              only down in an entry's details, that entry opens itself;
            - "Current" on the running version; "New" on every entry newer than the last
              one you had seen when you opened the log (nothing is marked the very first
              time, which would mark all of history; remembered in localStorage);
            - because the page is re-read with cache: 'no-cache' (a cheap conditional
              request when nothing changed), a deploy made since this page loaded shows up
              — with a note "A newer version is on the site", and a Reload button;
            - everything is inserted as TEXT: the entries talk about HTML and contain < and &.
          Two stray leftovers in the old log are handled rather than shown: ten bare
          version-number lines (e.g. a lone "1.29.40") from old editing, and the ==== rules.

          Limits: it reads the live page, so it needs the site served over http(s); opened
          straight from disk (file://) it says so instead of showing nothing. The older
          entries are engineering notes, so the summaries read that way; the log is shown
          as written, not rewritten. The panel is sized to stop above the player bar, which
          in this app floats above modal overlays (on a phone the list gets extra scroll
          room past it).

          Verified: 33 unit checks of the parser and the re-flow (test_updatelog.js, including
          the real log: every one of the 231 headers becomes exactly one entry, strictly
          descending, no stray lines) and 59 browser checks against the real served page
          (pw_updatelog.py: finding it, the full list and its order, month grouping, summaries
          vs an independent Python parse, details/HTML-as-text, search incl. details-only
          hits, expand/collapse all, closing four ways, the New badges across visits, a
          simulated newer deploy, a failed load with Retry, the file:// message, phone fit).
          Two rules were mutation-checked: dropping the version sort fails 4 checks, keeping
          the stray version lines fails 1.
          Not done: a "what's new" dot on the ⚙ button, per-version links, and plain-language
          rewrites of the old notes.

  1.29.184 — 2026-10-06 — Fix: a release's tracks (and cover) came from the WRONG iTunes
          album. Reported as: adding the album "Touch" by the artist "Touch" gave the
          tracks of a release called "Total Touch".

          Cause: three lookups asked iTunes for artist + title with limit=1 and trusted
          results[0] without checking it. iTunes orders results by relevance to the words
          typed, not by how closely a result IS the album asked for, and "Touch Touch" is
          also the heart of "Total Touch". The three were fetchArt (cover — which also
          cached the wrong album's collectionId), recoverFailedArt, and getTracksFor
          (tracks). A release that came from MusicBrainz already has a cover, so no id was
          cached and getTracksFor made its own blind search — hence wrong TRACKS, the part
          that was noticed.

          Fix: findItunesAlbum() asks for 15 results and accepts one only if its TITLE and
          ARTIST match the release:
            - titles compare after dropping bracketed edition text and a trailing "- EP" /
              "- Remastered 2011" style tail, folding case, accents, "&"/"and", leading
              "The" and punctuation — so "Abbey Road (Remastered)", "Touch - EP" and
              "Beatles"/"The Beatles" still match, while "Total Touch", "Touch Of Evil" and
              "Touché" do not. A real subtitle ("Live - Paris") is not mistaken for edition talk.
            - artists match if equal, or — for collaborations — share a credited artist.
            - when the plain album and a deluxe edition both qualify, the plain one wins.
          If "artist title" finds nothing that qualifies, a second query searches the title
          ALONE restricted to album titles and filters by artist (a short or common title can
          be buried or crowded out of the first). With no real match there is no iTunes
          match: the tracklist falls back to the release's own MusicBrainz link, else shows
          NO tracks — never a confident wrong one; the cover stays a placeholder.
          Nothing is saved: the art/track caches live only in the session, so a reload picks
          up the corrected matches for releases already in your crate.

          Trade-off: matching is deliberately strict. A release whose stored title differs
          from iTunes' (not just an edition suffix) now gets a placeholder cover where it
          used to get a loose guess — right when the guess is wrong, a small loss when it
          was right. The same applies to its tracklist unless it has a MusicBrainz link.

          Verified: 29 unit checks of the matching rules (test_itunes_match.js) and 16
          browser checks reproducing the report (pw_itunes_match.py: iTunes returns "Total
          Touch" first; real tracks, no lookup of Total Touch, MusicBrainz fallback, no
          tracks when there's no link, the second query, art, art recovery, an ordinary
          album, the quick peek). The browser test FAILS 11 of 16 against the pre-fix code
          with exactly the reported symptom (['TT One', 'TT Two']).
          Process note: my first attempt to prove that was cut off before it restored the
          fixed file, so for a while the suites ran against the OLD code (spotted because
          typeof findItunesAlbum was undefined) — everything was re-run against the
          verified fix. One older test (pw_peek_discs) stubbed iTunes with bare
          {collectionId} objects, which real iTunes never returns; it now returns names.

  1.29.183 — 2026-10-06 — Add a release: the pressing picker now sets the ORIGINAL
          issue in bold. When a release's pressings are listed (reached from the
          discography's "+ Add", from a free-text search result, or when adding the
          playing Spotify album — all one function, renderReleasePickerResults), the
          earliest official pressing(s) are bold, with a one-line key above the list
          ("Bold: the earliest official pressing(s)") and a hover tip on the row. The
          list stays most-recent-first on purpose (it surfaces the widely available
          reissue first), which leaves the original near the BOTTOM of a long list —
          exactly where bold helps. List rows: title and the year · label line go bold
          (the line is dim otherwise, so it also goes full-strength; the catalog number
          keeps its normal weight). Cover-grid mode: titles are already semi-bold, so
          the title steps up and the line under it goes bold and bright; the key spans
          the grid instead of taking a card's slot.

          What "original" means (earliestPressingIds):
            - MusicBrainz dates are partial ('1970' / '1970-03' / '1970-03-12'); a
              missing month/day counts as the start of the period, so they sort.
            - Only OFFICIAL pressings compete (a bootleg or promo that predates the
              real release isn't the original issue); no status counts as official; if
              nothing is official (all bootlegs) the dated ones compete instead.
            - Undated pressings can't be compared and never win; fewer than two dated
              candidates marks nothing (there'd be nothing to tell apart).
            - Pressings TIED for the earliest date are all marked (a simultaneous UK/US
              release has no single original) — unless every candidate ties, which says
              nothing, so nothing is marked.
          Plain free-text search results are NOT marked: those rows are different
          releases, not pressings of one. The order, the click behaviour and the data
          that lands in the form are unchanged.

          Verified in real Chromium (26 checks, MusicBrainz stubbed to the real release
          shape): the typical case (two same-day official pressings marked; the
          earlier bootleg and the undated one are not), unchanged order, key/tooltip,
          computed font weights in list and grid, partial dates, single / all-tied /
          one-dated lists (nothing marked), all-bootleg and no-status lists, an earlier
          promo, the key spanning the grid, clicking a marked pressing carries ITS
          MusicBrainz link into the form, drilling in from a search result, phone width.
          Two rules were mutation-checked (ignoring status fails 8 checks; marking when
          everything ties fails 1). Not verified: the real MusicBrainz service.

  1.29.182 — 2026-10-05 — Right-click a track in the Up Next queue. It had no
          context menu at all. The menu (also reachable with the keyboard's Menu key)
          for an UPCOMING track:
              Play now · Play next · Remove from queue
              Add to playlist ▸
              Go to artist · Go to album · View credits
          For the track that is PLAYING, the queue actions are left out (nothing to
          remove or skip to); for a track that already played, same. The row you
          right-clicked is marked with the accent bar while the menu is open. Works
          in all three queue views (thumbnail, track detail, release and tracks): every
          row now carries its position in the queue (data-queue-pos) — assigned after
          rendering from DOM order rather than by editing three long templates, and
          skipped (not guessed) if a row count ever disagrees with the queue. Over a
          heading or empty space the browser's own menu is left alone.

          Built on the player's right-click menu: that function is now a thin wrapper
          over a shared openTrackActionsMenu(), so the playlist submenu, artist, album
          and credits actions are the same code in both places (the player menu's 45
          checks still pass unchanged). The "something is already playing" placeholder
          at the head of a queue isn't a crate track, so right-clicking it gives the
          player's menu for what's playing.
            - The menu remembers the ENTRY, not a position: the queue can advance or be
              edited while it's open, so "Remove"/"Play next" re-find the entry and do
              nothing if it has since started playing.
            - Remove: only an upcoming entry. Safe against everything else that touches
              the queue — it advances by playCrateQueueIndex(id, index + 1) from the playing
              track and appends only ever add at the end (the multi-release loader
              included); nothing pre-loads upcoming tracks into Spotify.
            - Play next: moves the entry to directly after the playing one. With Shuffle
              on, entries carry an original-order number that turning Shuffle OFF sorts
              the rest of the queue back by; the moved entry takes one just below the
              tail's smallest, or switching Shuffle off would quietly put it back where it
              came from (the test fails without that line — checked).

          REGRESSION FROM 1.29.178, FOUND AND FIXED: in the floating Album Quick Peek,
          1.29.178 re-ran its keep-it-on-screen positioning after the tracklist loaded.
          For any card in the lower part of the window that pushed the peek UP OVER ITS OWN
          CARD, so the second click of a double-click (which plays the album) landed on the
          peek instead — double-click-to-play silently stopped working for those cards in
          the floating style. Found because this test double-clicks the Exuma card. The
          peek is now placed once, at open, so it never covers its card: below the card
          when there's room, otherwise above it, with its height capped to the room on
          that side (the track list scrolls inside). That also removes the visible jump.
          New tests cover EVERY card in the grid (never overlaps the card, always on
          screen, goes below or above as needed), double-click on the lowest card, and a
          deliberately short window.

          Not done: Spotify's own queue (the mini player's popover list) — its rows are
          Spotify tracks with no crate release behind them, so the crate actions don't
          apply; it needs a different menu. Verified in real Chromium (38 checks in
          pw_queue_menu, run three times because shuffle is random; 41 in
          pw_peek_details incl. the new placement ones).

  1.29.180 — 2026-10-04 — First step towards a Discover section: "Beyond your
          crate", under the in-crate row on the Artist Information page's
          Similar Artists tab. Last.fm's "listeners of X also play" (artist.
          getSimilar), minus every artist you already own, highest match first,
          up to 12, each with its match % and a link to its Last.fm page; click a
          tile to search that artist on Spotify. Uses the API key already saved
          for the Last.fm import. It's the one real taste-matching signal still
          available — Spotify's related-artists / recommendations / audio-analysis
          endpoints are closed to new apps.

          - Asked only when the Similar Artists tab is opened, and it never holds
            up the in-crate row (that appears at once; this fills in).
          - A READ-ONLY method, so it needs only the key — not the signed,
            write-capable lastfmApiCall, which also throws the error reason away.
            A small dedicated fetch tells the cases apart, each with its own
            message: no key (button opens the Last.fm dialog) / key rejected
            (Last.fm answers 403 with the reason in the body, so the body is read
            before looking at the status) / artist unknown to Last.fm / rate-limited
            or unreachable (Retry) / "everything it suggests is already in your
            crate" / no similar artists.
          - Cached 14 days in localStorage. Only "ok" and "artist unknown" are
            cached: a missing/rejected key or a network failure is retried the next
            time, so fixing the key takes effect immediately. Simultaneous lookups
            for the same artist share one request (re-rendering the tab used to fire
            two before the first answered).
          - Last.fm no longer has artist pictures, so each tile borrows an album
            cover from iTunes — but only when iTunes says the album is BY that
            artist (a fuzzy search for an obscure name happily returns someone
            else's record; a wrong picture is worse than a letter). One lookup at a
            time, spaced out.
          - Names and URLs from Last.fm are untrusted: names are escaped (a name
            that looks like HTML shows as text) and a link is only rendered if it
            points at https://www.last.fm/. Real links inside clickable tiles just
            follow themselves (they no longer also trigger the tile).

          Verified in real Chromium (36 checks) with Last.fm and iTunes stubbed to
          their documented response shapes: request parameters (unsigned,
          autocorrect, key), exclusion of owned artists, ordering, the 12 cap,
          HTML/javascript: in names and URLs, artwork verified vs mismatched, click
          vs link, in-memory and after-reload caching, every outcome above, a slow
          answer for an artist you've since left, phone width. Two new checks were
          confirmed to FAIL with their fix removed (the concurrent-request sharing).
          NOT verified: the real Last.fm and iTunes services (no network here), and
          — the real question — how GOOD the suggestions are for your library.
          Last.fm's similar-artist data leans towards popular acts; an obscure crate
          may get thin or odd results. That can only be judged on your own account.

          Not done (the rest of the Discover idea): "missing from artists you love"
          (unowned albums by your most-played/rated artists, from MusicBrainz) and
          "unexplored corners of a genre" (neighbouring genres in your taxonomy
          with nothing in your crate), and a place of their own to browse them.

  1.29.179 — 2026-10-04 — Album Quick Peek: more per-track details, wherever the
          source has them.
            - "DISC 1 / DISC 2" dividers before the first track of each disc, for
              releases that actually have more than one (a one-disc album gets no
              pointless "Disc 1"). Track numbers still run on continuously across
              discs, as before. Decided from the whole release, so a search that
              leaves only disc-2 tracks still says "Disc 2".
            - an "E" badge right after the title of explicit tracks (iTunes'
              trackExplicitness = 'explicit'; "cleaned" and "notExplicit" get none).
              It survives a long title: the ellipsis is on the title text, not the row.
          Sources: iTunes already supplied discNumber / discCount /
          trackExplicitness (the tracklist keeps the whole iTunes object) — they
          just weren't shown. The MusicBrainz fallback DISCARDED the disc (it only
          kept number/name/length, renumbering continuously across media); it now
          also keeps discNumber and discCount, so MusicBrainz-only releases show
          their discs too. MusicBrainz has no explicit flag, so none is invented.
          A hand-typed tracklist has neither field and looks exactly as before.

          Two bugs found while testing this, both fixed:
          1. (mine, from 1.29.178) A VERY long title broke its row apart: the title
             jumped onto a line of its own and dragged the length onto a third.
             1.29.178 made rows flex-wrap so details could drop to a second line, and
             a wrapping flex item is placed by its full content width, so any long
             title wrapped instead of being cut with an ellipsis. Caught by looking at
             a screenshot — every test title was short. Title now has flex-basis 0
             (it can never force a wrap) and two new checks assert a long title stays
             on one line in both peek styles; confirmed they FAIL with the fix
             reverted (row 72px tall instead of one line).
          2. (pre-existing) The INLINE peek opens in the grid's flow under the
             clicked row, and nothing ever scrolled it into view: for a card in the
             bottom visible row it opened entirely below the fold and looked like
             nothing had happened. It is now scrolled into view once its tracks have
             loaded (it only has its real height then), with the least scrolling
             that shows it.

          Not done: the same disc/explicit details on the album page's own
          tracklist, the Album and Tracks view and the Artist Information page's
          track lists (this change is the Album Quick Peek, inline and floating).
          Duration is shown wherever the source supplies one — hand-typed albums
          still have none unless a length is set in Edit track properties (an
          existing field), which is a separate question.
          Verified in real Chromium (26 checks, iTunes and MusicBrainz responses
          stubbed to their real shapes): disc order from a shuffled source,
          divider placement, continuous numbering, badge placement/long titles,
          one-disc and hand-typed albums unchanged, MusicBrainz discs kept in the
          cache, range-select across a divider, playing a row after one, floating
          style, scroll-into-view, phone width. Not verified: the real iTunes and
          MusicBrainz services (no network here).

  1.29.177 — 2026-10-04 — The tabs are now their own bar at the very top of the
          window, modeled on MusicBee's tab row, instead of sitting in the
          CRATE header. Full width, flush to the top edge, above the logo row:
              ←  →   MUSIC  DIARY  SCROBBLES  STATS  ARTIST  [artist tabs]  +
          Flat uppercase labels, the active tab brighter with an accent
          underline (rather than boxed pills).

          - ← / → are real history, like a browser's: every tab you land on is
            recorded (for an artist tab, which artist), going back and then
            somewhere new discards "forward", and an artist tab you've since
            closed is skipped rather than reopened. Disabled at the ends.
          - + opens a small finder to open any artist in your crate as a new tab
            (type to narrow — names that start with what you typed first — arrow
            keys + Enter, Escape or a click elsewhere to dismiss, the + toggles
            it). Artist tabs are the one kind of tab there is more than one of.
          - Artist tabs now open AFTER the fixed tabs, just before the +, as in
            MusicBee (they used to sit between MUSIC and DIARY).
          - A Player Controls bar docked at the TOP sits above the strip with the
            strip directly beneath it, as MusicBee's transport does.
          - On a phone nothing changes: it's the same .nav-tabs element, still the
            fixed bottom bar with its solid accent pill; the strip dissolves
            (display: contents) and its arrows/+ aren't shown.
          - The two window-sized pages (ARTIST and Artist Information) subtract the
            strip's height so they still fit without a page scrollbar.

          Not wired into ⚙ > Panels configuration: it's a fixed part of the window
          like the docked player (hiding it would leave no way to navigate, and on
          a phone it IS the navigation). If you want it listed there, say so.

          Two bugs found on the way, both fixed:
          1. Closing an artist tab you were NOT on threw you to MUSIC.
             activeArtistInfoTab is the last artist tab you were on and stays set
             after you've gone off to Diary/Stats, so the close took the "I closed
             the tab I'm looking at" branch. Closing a background tab now leaves
             you where you are, as in a browser.
          2. (Caught by my own docked-top test, before shipping) my first override
             of #app's top padding lost to a later, equally specific safe-area rule
             and left an 11px gap under the player; the test now allows at most 3px.

          Verified in real Chromium (55 checks): position (y=0, full width, above
          the header), order and styling, every tab still switching its page,
          history incl. artist tabs and closed ones, the + finder (keyboard,
          mouse, empty/no-match), page heights, the docked-top case and clicking
          through it, and the phone bottom bar. Not tested: touch devices with a
          notch/safe-area at the TOP edge on desktop-width layouts (iPad
          landscape) — the strip sits at y=0 there.

  1.29.176 — 2026-10-04 — New Main-panel element: Artist Biography, the strip
          under the grid in the MusicBee reference screenshot. An "Artist
          Biography" bar, a centred "Simon & Garfunkel  [ 8 plays ]" title,
          the picture on the left (Wikipedia photo, else the artist's cover)
          and the biography on the right, scrolling inside the strip rather
          than growing it. Add it from ⚙ > Panels configuration (it isn't on
          by default, so nothing changes unless you add it); Main panel only;
          no "Panel Settings…" button since there's nothing to configure.

          Which artist it shows — what you're BROWSING beats what's playing:
          the artist picked/filtered in the left panel, a picked album's
          artist, or a search that has narrowed the grid to a single artist;
          only if none of those applies, the artist of the track in the player
          (including a Spotify track that isn't in the crate). Clicking the
          name or the picture opens that artist's full Artist Information page.

          Same data and rules as the Artist Information page's Profile tab —
          one shared lookup (aiExt / aiEnsureProfile: MusicBrainz's own link
          to the artist's Wikipedia page, cached, never a text search), so an
          artist loaded in either place is instantly available in the other.
          Every outcome has its own message: no Wikipedia link, not on
          MusicBrainz, same-named artists (with a button that opens the page
          on More Albums, where that's settled), unreachable (with Retry).
          Lookups are DEBOUNCED 600ms: clicking through five artists in a row
          looks up only the one you stopped on, instead of queueing the
          MusicBrainz requests for four you'd already moved past (tested: 5
          artists clicked in one go -> exactly 1 search). A slow response for
          an artist you've left can't overwrite the one you're on, and going
          back to a loaded artist costs no new requests.

          On a phone the picture sits small BESIDE the text instead of above
          it: stacked, the strip was ~500px tall and squeezed the album grid
          to a sliver (caught from a screenshot, not a test).

          MusicBrainz/Wikidata/Wikipedia response shapes): enabling and

          the grid, the left-panel/playing/search priority order, all five
          states, the debounce and the stale-response race, reload
          persistence of the layout, phone fit. Not verified: the real
          services (no network here).

  1.29.175 — 2026-10-04 — Artist Information page redesigned to match the
          MusicBee "Music Explorer" reference screenshots (it was a bare
          stats line over a grid of covers). Each artist tab is now a full
          page:
            - left: an "Album Artist" list of every artist in the crate
              (thumbnail + "N albums", filterable, current one highlighted;
              clicking another artist opens/switches to their tab);
            - header: round photo, large name, a ▶ that plays everything by
              the artist (discography order, honours Shuffle), "1 play · 1
              album/ 7 tracks" exactly as in the reference, and a
              "playing now:" line when what's loaded in the player is theirs;
            - four sub-tabs, with the decade and main genre ("1970s · Freak
              Folk") at the right of the bar:
              Albums & Stats  owned releases as tiles (click = album page,
                              double-click = play, right-click = the usual
                              album menu) + TOP TRACKS with per-track play
                              counts (scrobbles), a Top tracks / Album order
                              switch, and TOTAL PLAY COUNT; double-click a
                              track plays it, right-click opens the track menu.
              More Albums     the artist's MusicBrainz discography as cover
                              tiles (Cover Art Archive art, placeholder if
                              missing), owned ones marked, "+ Add" on the
                              rest, "Show everything" for singles/comps,
                              a MusicBrainz link, and the same-named-artist
                              picker.
              Profile         photo + biography from Wikipedia with Read
                              More…, a CC BY-SA credit and link, and Top
                              Tracks (album, time, plays) with View All.
              Similar Artists other artists IN YOUR CRATE ranked by genre
                              overlap (position-weighted like the rest of
                              the app, ancestors in the genre tree at half
                              weight, shared scene/movement counts). Entirely
                              local — no service or API key.

          How the biography is found: MusicBrainz's own links for the artist
          (a direct Wikipedia link, or Wikidata -> its English Wikipedia
          title), then one Wikipedia query for text + photo. Never a text
          search, so a common name can't pull in a different person's page; if
          MusicBrainz has no link it says so and offers a Wikipedia search
          instead. Results are cached in localStorage (30 days found, 7 days
          "none"; a transient failure is never cached). Reuses
          fetchArtistDiscography for the artist match, so same-named artists
          are settled the same way as on the ARTIST tab.

          THREE PRE-EXISTING BUGS FOUND AND FIXED while building this (all in
          code the old ARTIST tab already shared):
          1. Same-named artists never got the "which one is this?" picker. An
             ambiguous result has no `items`, and `items == null` was tested
             first, so the ARTIST tab showed "Couldn't reach MusicBrainz right
             now" + Retry forever instead. Reproduced against the delivered
             v1.29.174 before changing anything. Fixed in the ARTIST tab and
             in the new page.
          2. fetchArtistDiscography reported a FAILED release lookup as "this
             artist has no releases" and cached that for the session. A failed
             lookup is now "unreachable" (not cached, Retry really retries).
          3. ...and an artist MusicBrainz simply doesn't have (common in a
             personal crate) came back as "unreachable"; it is now a real,
             cacheable "not found", with its own message on the new page.

          Other things the testing caught: getGenreList returns the
          placeholder genre 'Unknown' for a release with no genre, which made
          two untagged artists rank as identical and put "2000s · Unknown" in
          the header — real tags only now (aiGenresOf); the page shrank to
          ~790px because a flex item with auto margins fits its contents
          (width: 100% now); on a phone the sub-tab bar clipped "Similar
          Artists" (tightened to fit the ~308px the page gets there).
          computeArtistStats' track count now also uses fetched tracklists
          (trackCache), not only manually-typed ones, so releases added by
          lookup no longer read as 0 tracks. Async lookups (tracklists,
          discography, profile) re-check they're still the page on screen
          before painting — tested with a deliberately slow response for the
          artist you've already left.

          Verified in real Chromium (90 checks) with the network stubbed to
          the real MusicBrainz/Wikidata/Wikipedia response shapes: layout
          against the reference, every sub-tab, all interactions, failure /
          not-found / ambiguous / empty paths, reload persistence of the
          biography cache, a stale-response race, a phone-sized screen.
          NOT verified: the real MusicBrainz, Wikidata, Wikipedia and Cover
          Art Archive services (no network here) — the response shapes come
          from their documented formats, but an unexpected difference would
          only show up live. Not done: a "Similar Artists" source beyond your
          own crate (e.g. Last.fm, which needs an API key); the reference's
          "Get it on iTunes" badge (replaced by a MusicBrainz link); a
          waveform/spectrum like MusicBee's player chrome.

  1.29.174 — 2026-10-03 — Feature: right-click the player's track (art,
          title, artist or album) for a menu on whatever is playing:
            Add to playlist ▸   (the existing playlist flyout)
            Add to queue        (the existing cross-platform queue)
            Go to artist        (opens that artist's Artist Info tab)
            Go to album         (opens the album page)
            View credits        (new popover, see below)

          Works whichever engine is playing. The old right-click menu existed
          only on the album NAME and only for Spotify (it keyed off
          currentSpotifyTrack), so it did nothing for a YouTube/SoundCloud
          track or one played from the crate queue; it is replaced
          (openAlbumContextMenu removed — it had one caller). The playing
          track is resolved by resolveNowPlayingForMenu(): the crate queue's
          own current entry first (exact release, name and track number for
          every engine), then nowPlayingItem, then — for a Spotify track
          played from outside the crate — a lookup by artist+album.
          Track number comes from the cached tracklist or a manual album's
          own list (lookupTrackNum); if it isn't known yet the tracklist is
          fetched in the background and filled in on the same object the
          playlist flyout later sends to the server.

          Not in your crate (a Spotify track you haven't added): the menu
          still opens, offers "Add album to your crate…" first (the existing
          add flow), and shows the crate-only items GREYED OUT with a "Not in
          your crate" tooltip rather than hiding them. "Go to artist" stays
          enabled if you own other releases by that artist.

          box.

          Also: Escape now closes these floating menus (it closed deck/modals
          but not these). Only swallows the browser's own right-click menu
          when something is actually playing. Queuing the playing track while
          it plays OUTSIDE any queue follows addToCrateQueue's existing
          behaviour (the playing track becomes the queue's first entry, the
          queued one follows it).

          Tested in real Chromium (45 checks): the menu for a crate-queue
          track, YouTube played outside the queue, Spotify matched to the
          crate, Spotify not in the crate, and Spotify with a known artist
          only; queue append; the playlist POST (right row/track/number);

          outside-click dismissal; player docked top and bottom; phone width;
          nothing-playing leaves the browser menu alone.

          Not covered: right-clicking the YouTube VIDEO face itself (a cross-
          origin iframe swallows the event — use the art/title beside it);
          touch long-press on a real phone (only a synthetic contextmenu event
          was tried); real Spotify playback (a stand-in current track was
          used). "Go to artist" opens the Artist Info tab, as the album quick
          peek's artist link does — the player's own artist-NAME click still
          filters the Music tab to that artist instead, a pre-existing
